<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about William Pitt the Elder. He is not to be confused with <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a>.</div>

src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Flag_of_Great_Britain_%281707%E2%80%931800%29.svg/23px-Flag_of_Great_Britain_%281707%E2%80%931800%29.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="14" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Flag_of_Great_Britain_%281707%E2%80%931800%29.svg/35px-Flag_of_Great_Britain_%281707%E2%80%931800%29.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Flag_of_Great_Britain_%281707%E2%80%931800%29.svg/46px-Flag_of_Great_Britain_%281707%E2%80%931800%29.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" class="infobox-label">Branch/service</th><td class="infobox-data"><span class="flagicon"><span class="mw-image-border" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Flag_of_the_British_Army.svg/23px-Flag_of_the_British_Army.svg.png" decoding="async" width="23" height="13" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Flag_of_the_British_Army.svg/35px-Flag_of_the_British_Army.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Flag_of_the_British_Army.svg/46px-Flag_of_the_British_Army.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="710" data-file-height="406" /></span></span>&#160;</span><a href="/wiki/British_Army" title="British Army">British Army</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Unit</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/1st_King%27s_Dragoon_Guards" title="1st King&#39;s Dragoon Guards">King's Own Regiment of Horse</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right; font-size: 95%"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>William Pitt, 1st Earl of Chatham</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (15 November 1708&#160;&#8211;&#32;11 May 1778) was a British <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whig</a> statesman who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Great_Britain" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime Minister of Great Britain">Prime Minister of Great Britain</a> from 1766 to 1768. Historians call him <b>Chatham</b> or <b>William Pitt the Elder</b> to distinguish him from his son <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a>, who was also a prime minister. Pitt was also known as <b>the Great Commoner</b>, because of his long-standing refusal to accept a title until 1766.
</p><p>Pitt was a member of the British cabinet and its informal leader from 1756 to 1761 (with a brief interlude in 1757), during the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a> (including the <a href="/wiki/French_and_Indian_War" title="French and Indian War">French and Indian War</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Thirteen_Colonies" title="Thirteen Colonies">American colonies</a>). He again led the ministry, holding the official title of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a>, between 1766 and 1768. Much of his power came from his brilliant oratory.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> He was out of power for most of his career and became well known for his attacks on the government, such as those on <a href="/wiki/Robert_Walpole" title="Robert Walpole">Walpole</a>'s corruption in the 1730s, Hanoverian subsidies in the 1740s, <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1763)" title="Treaty of Paris (1763)">peace with France in the 1760s</a>, and the uncompromising policy towards the American colonies in the 1770s.<sup id="cite_ref-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt is best known as the wartime political leader of Britain in the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a>, especially for his single-minded devotion to victory over France, a victory which ultimately solidified Britain's dominance over world affairs. He is also known for his popular appeal, his opposition to corruption in government, his support for the American position in the run-up to the <a href="/wiki/American_Revolutionary_War" title="American Revolutionary War">American Revolutionary War</a>, his advocacy of British greatness, expansionism and empire, and his antagonism towards Britain's chief enemies and rivals for colonial power, <a href="/wiki/Enlightenment_in_Spain" title="Enlightenment in Spain">Spain</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_France" title="Kingdom of France">France</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Marie Peters argues his statesmanship was based on a clear, consistent, and distinct appreciation of the value of the Empire.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The British parliamentary historian <a href="/wiki/P._D._G._Thomas" title="P. D. G. Thomas">P. D. G. Thomas</a> argued that Pitt's power was based not on his family connections but on the extraordinary parliamentary skills by which he dominated the House of Commons. He displayed a commanding manner, brilliant rhetoric, and sharp debating skills that cleverly utilised broad literary and historical knowledge.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Scholars rank him highly among all British prime ministers.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Family">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Pitt was the grandson of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pitt" title="Thomas Pitt">Thomas Pitt</a> (1653–1726), the governor of <a href="/wiki/Madras" class="mw-redirect" title="Madras">Madras</a>, known as "Diamond" Pitt for having discovered <a href="/wiki/Regent_Diamond" title="Regent Diamond">a diamond</a> of extraordinary size and sold it to the <a href="/wiki/Philip_II,_Duke_of_Orl%C3%A9ans" class="mw-redirect" title="Philip II, Duke of Orléans">Duke of Orléans</a> for around £135,000.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> This transaction, as well as other trading deals in <a href="/wiki/British_India" class="mw-redirect" title="British India">India</a>, established the Pitt family fortune. After returning home the Governor was able to raise his family to a position of wealth and political influence: in 1691 he purchased the property of <a href="/wiki/Boconnoc" title="Boconnoc">Boconnoc</a> in <a href="/wiki/Cornwall" title="Cornwall">Cornwall</a>, which gave him control of a <a href="/wiki/Okehampton_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Okehampton (UK Parliament constituency)">seat in Parliament</a>. He made further land purchases and became one of the dominant political figures in the <a href="/wiki/West_Country" title="West Country">West Country</a>, controlling seats such as the <a href="/wiki/Rotten_borough" class="mw-redirect" title="Rotten borough">rotten borough</a> of <a href="/wiki/Old_Sarum_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Old Sarum (UK Parliament constituency)">Old Sarum</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19111_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19111-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomaspitt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/6/62/Thomaspitt.jpg" decoding="async" width="200" height="255" class="mw-file-element" data-file-width="200" data-file-height="255" /></a><figcaption>Governor <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pitt" title="Thomas Pitt">Thomas "Diamond" Pitt</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>William's father was <a href="/wiki/Robert_Pitt" title="Robert Pitt">Robert Pitt</a> (1680–1727), the eldest son of Governor Pitt, who served as a <a href="/wiki/Tory" title="Tory">Tory</a> <a href="/wiki/Member_of_Parliament_(United_Kingdom)" title="Member of Parliament (United Kingdom)">Member of Parliament</a> from 1705 to 1727. His mother was Harriet Villiers,<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Villiers-FitzGerald" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward Villiers-FitzGerald">Edward Villiers-FitzGerald</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Ireland" title="Kingdom of Ireland">Irish</a> <a href="/wiki/Beneficiary" title="Beneficiary">heiress</a> <a href="/wiki/Katherine_FitzGerald,_Viscountess_Grandison" title="Katherine FitzGerald, Viscountess Grandison">Katherine FitzGerald</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Both William's paternal uncles <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Londonderry" title="Thomas Pitt, 1st Earl of Londonderry">Thomas</a> and <a href="/wiki/John_Pitt_(colonel)" class="mw-redirect" title="John Pitt (colonel)">John</a> were MPs, while his aunt <a href="/wiki/Lucy_Stanhope,_Countess_of_Stanhope" class="mw-redirect" title="Lucy Stanhope, Countess of Stanhope">Lucy</a> married the leading <a href="/wiki/Whig_(British_political_party)" class="mw-redirect" title="Whig (British political party)">Whig</a> politician and soldier General <a href="/wiki/James_Stanhope,_1st_Earl_Stanhope" title="James Stanhope, 1st Earl Stanhope">James Stanhope</a>. From 1717 to 1721, Stanhope served as effective First Minister in the <a href="/wiki/First_Stanhope%E2%80%93Sunderland_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Stanhope–Sunderland Ministry">Stanhope–Sunderland Ministry</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> and was a useful political contact for the Pitt family until the collapse of the <a href="/wiki/South_Sea_Bubble" class="mw-redirect" title="South Sea Bubble">South Sea Bubble</a>, a disaster which engulfed the government.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Birth_and_Education">Birth and Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Birth and Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>William Pitt was born at <a href="/wiki/Golden_Square" title="Golden Square">Golden Square</a>, <a href="/wiki/Westminster" title="Westminster">Westminster</a>, on 15 November 1708.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> His older brother <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pitt_of_Boconnoc" title="Thomas Pitt of Boconnoc">Thomas Pitt</a> had been born in 1704. There were also five sisters: Harriet, Catherine, Ann, Elizabeth, and Mary. From 1719 William was educated at <a href="/wiki/Eton_College" title="Eton College">Eton College</a> along with his brother. William disliked Eton, later claiming that "a <a href="/wiki/Independent_school" class="mw-redirect" title="Independent school">public school</a> might suit a boy of turbulent disposition but would not do where there was any gentleness".<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> It was at school that Pitt began to suffer from <a href="/wiki/Gout" title="Gout">gout</a>. Governor Pitt died in 1726, and the family estate at Boconnoc passed to William's father. When he died the following year, Boconnoc was inherited by William's elder brother, <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pitt_of_Boconnoc" title="Thomas Pitt of Boconnoc">Thomas Pitt of Boconnoc</a>.
</p><p>In January 1727, William was entered as a <a href="/wiki/Gentleman_commoner" class="mw-redirect" title="Gentleman commoner">gentleman commoner</a> at <a href="/wiki/Trinity_College,_Oxford" title="Trinity College, Oxford">Trinity College, Oxford</a>. There is evidence that he was an extensive reader, if not a minutely accurate <a href="/wiki/Classics" title="Classics">classical</a> scholar. <a href="/wiki/Virgil" title="Virgil">Virgil</a> was his favourite author. William diligently cultivated the faculty of expression by the practice of translation and re-translation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19111_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19111-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In these years he became a close friend of <a href="/wiki/George_Lyttelton,_1st_Baron_Lyttelton" title="George Lyttelton, 1st Baron Lyttelton">George Lyttelton</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> who would later become a leading politician. In 1728 a violent attack of gout compelled him to leave Oxford without finishing his degree. He then chose to travel abroad, from 1728 attending <a href="/wiki/Utrecht_University" title="Utrecht University">Utrecht University</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Republic" title="Dutch Republic">Dutch Republic</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> gaining a knowledge of <a href="/wiki/Hugo_Grotius" title="Hugo Grotius">Hugo Grotius</a> and other writers on international law and diplomacy. It is not known how long Pitt studied at Utrecht;<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> by 1730 he had returned to his brother's estate at Boconnoc.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He had recovered from the attack of gout, but the disease proved intractable, and he continued to be subject to attacks of growing intensity at frequent intervals until his death.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_career">Military career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Military career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Richard_Temple,_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Richard_Temple%2C_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg/220px-Richard_Temple%2C_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="267" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Richard_Temple%2C_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg/330px-Richard_Temple%2C_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Richard_Temple%2C_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg/440px-Richard_Temple%2C_1st_Viscount_Cobham_by_Jean_Baptiste_van_Loo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2913" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Richard_Temple,_1st_Viscount_Cobham" title="Richard Temple, 1st Viscount Cobham">Lord Cobham</a>, Pitt's commanding officer and political mentor. Pitt was part of a group of young MPs known as <a href="/wiki/Cobham%27s_Cubs" class="mw-redirect" title="Cobham&#39;s Cubs">Cobham's Cubs</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>On Pitt's return home it was necessary for him, as the younger son, to choose a profession and he opted for a career in the army.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> He obtained a <a href="/wiki/Cornet_(military_rank)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cornet (military rank)">cornet</a>'s commission in the <a href="/wiki/Dragoons" class="mw-redirect" title="Dragoons">dragoons</a> with the King's Own Regiment of Horse (later <a href="/wiki/1st_King%27s_Dragoon_Guards" title="1st King&#39;s Dragoon Guards">1st King's Dragoon Guards</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain" title="George II of Great Britain">George II</a> never forgot the jibes of "the terrible cornet of horse".<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> It was reported that the <a href="/wiki/Purchase_of_commissions_in_the_British_Army" title="Purchase of commissions in the British Army">£1,000 cost of the commission</a> had been supplied by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Walpole" title="Robert Walpole">Robert Walpole</a>, the prime minister, out of <a href="/wiki/HM_Treasury" title="HM Treasury">Treasury</a> funds in an attempt to secure the support of Pitt's brother Thomas in Parliament. Alternatively the fee may have been waived by the commanding officer of the regiment, <a href="/wiki/Richard_Temple,_1st_Viscount_Cobham" title="Richard Temple, 1st Viscount Cobham">Lord Cobham</a>, who was related to the Pitt brothers by marriage.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt was to grow close to Cobham, whom he regarded as almost a surrogate father. He was stationed for much of his service in <a href="/wiki/Northampton" title="Northampton">Northampton</a>, on peacetime duties. Pitt was particularly frustrated that, owing to Walpole's isolationist policies, Britain had not entered the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Polish_Succession" title="War of the Polish Succession">War of the Polish Succession</a> which began in 1733, and he had not been able to test himself in battle.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Pitt was granted extended leave in 1733, and toured France and Switzerland. He briefly visited Paris but spent most of his time in the French provinces,<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> spending the winter in <a href="/wiki/Lun%C3%A9ville" title="Lunéville">Lunéville</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Duchy_of_Lorraine" title="Duchy of Lorraine">Duchy of Lorraine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt's military career was destined to be relatively short. His elder brother Thomas was returned at the <a href="/wiki/1734_British_general_election" title="1734 British general election">general election of 1734</a> for two separate seats, <a href="/wiki/Okehampton_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Okehampton (UK Parliament constituency)">Okehampton</a> and <a href="/wiki/Old_Sarum_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Old Sarum (UK Parliament constituency)">Old Sarum</a>, and chose to sit for Okehampton, passing the vacant seat to William who accordingly, in February 1735, entered <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Great_Britain" title="Parliament of Great Britain">parliament</a> as member for Old Sarum.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> He became one of a large number of serving army officers in the House of Commons.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Rise_to_prominence">Rise to prominence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Rise to prominence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patriot_Whigs">Patriot Whigs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Patriot Whigs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Cobhamites" title="Cobhamites">Cobhamites</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder,_in_the_Guildhall,_London.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_in_the_Guildhall%2C_London.JPG/220px-The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_in_the_Guildhall%2C_London.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_in_the_Guildhall%2C_London.JPG/330px-The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_in_the_Guildhall%2C_London.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0d/The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_in_the_Guildhall%2C_London.JPG/440px-The_monument_to_William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_in_the_Guildhall%2C_London.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2056" data-file-height="3088" /></a><figcaption>The huge monument to William Pitt the Elder, in the <a href="/wiki/Guildhall,_London" title="Guildhall, London">Guildhall, London</a> stands opposite an equally huge monument to his son, William Pitt the Younger in a balanced composition</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pitt soon joined a faction of discontented <a href="/wiki/British_Whig_Party" class="mw-redirect" title="British Whig Party">Whigs</a> known as the <a href="/wiki/Patriot_Whigs" title="Patriot Whigs">Patriots</a> who formed part of the opposition. The group commonly met at <a href="/wiki/Stowe_House" title="Stowe House">Stowe House</a>, the country estate of <a href="/wiki/Richard_Temple,_1st_Viscount_Cobham" title="Richard Temple, 1st Viscount Cobham">Lord Cobham</a>, who was a leader of the group.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> Cobham had originally been a supporter of <a href="/wiki/Whig_government_1730%E2%80%9342" class="mw-redirect" title="Whig government 1730–42">the government</a> under Sir Robert Walpole, but a dispute over the controversial <a href="/wiki/Excise_Bill" title="Excise Bill">Excise Bill</a> of 1733 had seen them join the opposition. Pitt swiftly became one of the faction's most prominent members.
</p><p>Pitt's <a href="/wiki/Maiden_speech" title="Maiden speech">maiden speech</a> in the Commons was delivered in April 1736, in the debate on the congratulatory address to <a href="/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain" title="George II of Great Britain">George II</a> on the marriage of his son <a href="/wiki/Frederick,_Prince_of_Wales" title="Frederick, Prince of Wales">Frederick, Prince of Wales</a>. He used the occasion to pay compliments, and there was nothing striking in the speech as reported, but it helped to gain him the attention of the House when he later took part on debates on more partisan subjects.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In particular, he attacked Britain's non-intervention in the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Polish_Succession" title="War of the Polish Succession">ongoing European war</a>, which he believed was in violation of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Vienna_(1731)" title="Treaty of Vienna (1731)">Treaty of Vienna</a> and the terms of the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Austrian_Alliance" title="Anglo-Austrian Alliance">Anglo-Austrian Alliance</a>.
</p><p>He became such a troublesome critic of the government that Walpole moved to punish him by arranging his dismissal from the army in 1736, along with several of his friends and political allies. This provoked a wave of hostility to Walpole because many saw such an act as <a href="/wiki/Unconstitutional" class="mw-redirect" title="Unconstitutional">unconstitutional</a>—that members of Parliament were being dismissed for their <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_speech" title="Freedom of speech">freedom of speech</a> in attacking the government, something protected by <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_privilege" title="Parliamentary privilege">Parliamentary privilege</a>. None of the men had their commissions reinstated, however, and the incident brought an end to Pitt's military career.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The loss of Pitt's commission was soon compensated. The heir to the throne, <a href="/wiki/Frederick,_Prince_of_Wales" title="Frederick, Prince of Wales">Frederick, Prince of Wales</a>, was involved in a long-running dispute with his father, George II, and was the patron of the <a href="/wiki/Parliamentary_opposition" title="Parliamentary opposition">opposition</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> He appointed Pitt one of his <a href="/wiki/Groom_of_the_Chamber" title="Groom of the Chamber">Grooms of the Bedchamber</a> as a reward.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup> In this new position his hostility to the government did not in any degree relax, and his oratorical gifts were substantial.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War">War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: War">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Spanish_war">Spanish war</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Spanish war">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/War_of_Jenkins%27_Ear" title="War of Jenkins&#39; Ear">War of Jenkins' Ear</a></div>
<p>During the 1730s Britain's relationship with Spain had slowly declined. Repeated cases of reported Spanish mistreatment of British merchants, whom they accused of smuggling, caused public outrage, particularly the incident of <a href="/wiki/Jenkins%27_Ear" class="mw-redirect" title="Jenkins&#39; Ear">Jenkins' Ear</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> Pitt was a leading advocate of a more hard-line policy against Spain, and often castigated Walpole's government for its weakness in dealing with <a href="/wiki/Madrid" title="Madrid">Madrid</a>. Pitt spoke out against the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_El_Pardo_(1739)" class="mw-redirect" title="Treaty of El Pardo (1739)">Convention of El Pardo</a> which aimed to settle the dispute peacefully.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> In the speech against the Convention in the House of Commons on 8 March 1739 Pitt said:
</p>
<blockquote><p>When trade is at stake, it is your last entrenchment; you must defend it, or perish&#160; ... Sir, Spain knows the consequence of a war in America; whoever gains, it must prove fatal to her&#160; ... is this any longer a nation? Is this any longer an English Parliament, if with more ships in your harbours than in all the navies of Europe; with above two millions of people in your American colonies, you will bear to hear of the expediency of receiving from Spain an insecure, unsatisfactory, dishonourable Convention?<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Owing to public pressure, the British government was pushed towards declaring war with Spain in 1739. Britain began with a <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Porto_Bello_(1739)" title="Battle of Porto Bello (1739)">success at Porto Bello</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> However the war effort soon stalled, and Pitt alleged that the government was not prosecuting the war effectively—demonstrated by the fact that the British waited two years before taking further offensive action fearing that further British victories would provoke the French into declaring war.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> When they did so, <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Cartagena_de_Indias" title="Battle of Cartagena de Indias">a failed attack</a> was made on the South American port of <a href="/wiki/Cartagena,_Colombia" title="Cartagena, Colombia">Cartagena</a> which left thousands of British troops dead, over half from disease, and cost many ships. The decision to attack during the <a href="/wiki/Rainy_season" class="mw-redirect" title="Rainy season">rainy season</a> was held as further evidence of the government's incompetence.
</p><p>After this, the colonial war against Spain was almost entirely abandoned as British resources were switched towards fighting France in Europe as the <a href="/wiki/War_of_the_Austrian_Succession" title="War of the Austrian Succession">War of the Austrian Succession</a> had broken out. The Spanish had repelled a major invasion intended to conquer Central America and succeeded in maintaining their trans-Atlantic convoys while causing much disruption to British shipping and twice broke a British blockade to land troops in Italy, but the war with Spain was treated as a draw. Many of the underlying issues remained unresolved by the later peace treaties leaving the potential for future conflicts to occur. Pitt considered the war a missed opportunity to take advantage of a power in decline, although later he became an advocate of warmer relations with the Spanish in an effort to prevent them forming an alliance with France.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Hanover">Hanover</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Hanover">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg/220px-GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg/330px-GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fa/GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg/440px-GeorgeIIWootton1743.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3148" data-file-height="2353" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain" title="George II of Great Britain">George II</a> leading his forces to victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Dettingen" title="Battle of Dettingen">Battle of Dettingen</a> (1743). Pitt incurred his lasting displeasure by attacking British support for <a href="/wiki/Electorate_of_Hanover" title="Electorate of Hanover">Hanover</a>, which would blight their relations for twenty years.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Walpole and Newcastle were now giving the war in Europe a much higher priority than the colonial conflict with Spain in the Americas. <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Prussia" title="Kingdom of Prussia">Prussia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire" title="Holy Roman Empire">Austria</a> went to war in 1740, with many other European states soon joining in.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> There was a fear that France would launch an invasion of <a href="/wiki/Electorate_of_Hanover" title="Electorate of Hanover">Hanover</a>, which was linked to Britain through the crown of <a href="/wiki/George_II_of_Great_Britain" title="George II of Great Britain">George II</a>. To avert this Walpole and Newcastle decided to pay a large <a href="/wiki/Subsidy" title="Subsidy">subsidy</a> to both Austria and Hanover, in order for them to raise troops and defend themselves.
</p><p>Pitt now launched an attack on such subsidies, playing to widespread anti-Hanoverian feelings in Britain. This boosted his popularity with the public, but earned him the lifelong hatred of the King, who was emotionally committed to Hanover, where he had spent the first thirty years of his life. In response to Pitt's attacks, the British government decided not to pay a direct subsidy to Hanover, but instead to pass the money indirectly through Austria—a move which was considered more politically acceptable. A sizeable <a href="/wiki/Pragmatic_Army" title="Pragmatic Army">Anglo-German army</a> was formed which George II himself led to victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Dettingen" title="Battle of Dettingen">Battle of Dettingen</a> in 1743, reducing the immediate threat to Hanover.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Fall_of_Walpole">Fall of Walpole</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Fall of Walpole">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Many of Pitt's attacks on the government were directed personally at Walpole who had now been Prime Minister for twenty years. He spoke in favour of the motion in 1742 for an <a href="/wiki/Inquiry" title="Inquiry">inquiry</a> into the last ten years of Walpole's administration. In February 1742, following <a href="/wiki/1741_British_general_election" title="1741 British general election">poor election results</a> and the disaster at Cartagena, Walpole was at last forced to succumb to the long-continued attacks of opposition, resigned and took a <a href="/wiki/Peerage" title="Peerage">peerage</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt now expected a new government to be formed led by <a href="/wiki/William_Pulteney,_1st_Earl_of_Bath" title="William Pulteney, 1st Earl of Bath">Pulteney</a> and dominated by <a href="/wiki/Tories" class="mw-redirect" title="Tories">Tories</a> and <a href="/wiki/Patriot_Whigs" title="Patriot Whigs">Patriot Whigs</a> in which he could expect a junior position.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Walpole was instead succeeded as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister</a> by <a href="/wiki/Spencer_Compton,_1st_Earl_of_Wilmington" title="Spencer Compton, 1st Earl of Wilmington">Lord Wilmington</a>, though the real power in the new government was divided between <a href="/wiki/John_Carteret,_2nd_Earl_Granville" title="John Carteret, 2nd Earl Granville">Lord Carteret</a> and the Pelham brothers (<a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry</a> and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-upon-Tyne" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Pelham-Holles, 1st Duke of Newcastle-upon-Tyne">Thomas, Duke of Newcastle</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Walpole had carefully orchestrated this new government as a continuance of his own, and continued to advise it up to his death in 1745. Pitt's hopes for a place in the government were thwarted, and he remained in opposition. He was therefore unable to make any personal gain from the downfall of Walpole, to which he had personally contributed a great deal.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/First_Pelham_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Pelham Ministry">administration formed by the Pelhams</a> in 1744, after the dismissal of Carteret, included many of Pitt's former Patriot allies, but Pitt was not granted a position because of continued ill-feeling by the King and leading Whigs about his views on Hanover. In 1744 Pitt received a large boost to his personal fortune when the <a href="/wiki/Sarah_Churchill,_Duchess_of_Marlborough" title="Sarah Churchill, Duchess of Marlborough">Dowager Duchess of Marlborough</a> died leaving him a <a href="/wiki/Inheritance" title="Inheritance">legacy</a> of £10,000 as an "acknowledgment of the noble defence he had made for the support of the laws of England and to prevent the ruin of his country".<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> It was probably as much a mark of her dislike of Walpole as of her admiration of Pitt.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_government">In government</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: In government">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Paymaster_of_the_Forces">Paymaster of the Forces</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Paymaster of the Forces">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Pitt_the_Elder,_by_Joseph_Wilton,_National_Portrait_Gallery,_London.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_by_Joseph_Wilton%2C_National_Portrait_Gallery%2C_London.jpg/220px-William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_by_Joseph_Wilton%2C_National_Portrait_Gallery%2C_London.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_by_Joseph_Wilton%2C_National_Portrait_Gallery%2C_London.jpg/330px-William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_by_Joseph_Wilton%2C_National_Portrait_Gallery%2C_London.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_by_Joseph_Wilton%2C_National_Portrait_Gallery%2C_London.jpg/440px-William_Pitt_the_Elder%2C_by_Joseph_Wilton%2C_National_Portrait_Gallery%2C_London.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2056" data-file-height="3088" /></a><figcaption>William Pitt the Elder, by <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Wilton" title="Joseph Wilton">Joseph Wilton</a>, National Portrait Gallery, London</figcaption></figure>
<p>Reluctantly the King finally agreed to give Pitt a place in the government. Pitt had changed his stance on a number of issues to make himself more acceptable to George, most notably the heated issue of Hanoverian subsidies. To force the matter, the Pelham brothers had to resign on the question whether he should be admitted or not, and it was only after all other arrangements had proved impracticable, that they were reinstated with Pitt appointed as <a href="/wiki/Vice-Treasurer_of_Ireland" class="mw-redirect" title="Vice-Treasurer of Ireland">Vice-Treasurer of Ireland</a> in February 1746.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> George continued to resent him however.
</p><p>In May of the same year Pitt was promoted to the more important and lucrative office of <a href="/wiki/Paymaster_of_the_Forces" title="Paymaster of the Forces">paymaster-general</a>, which gave him a place in the <a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">Privy Council</a>, though not in the <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_(government)" title="Cabinet (government)">cabinet</a>. Here he had an opportunity of displaying his public spirit and integrity in a way that deeply impressed both the king and the country. It had been the usual practise of previous paymasters to appropriate to themselves the interest of all money lying in their hands by way of advance, and also to accept a commission of <style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1154941027">.mw-parser-output .frac{white-space:nowrap}.mw-parser-output .frac .num,.mw-parser-output .frac .den{font-size:80%;line-height:0;vertical-align:super}.mw-parser-output .frac .den{vertical-align:sub}.mw-parser-output .sr-only{border:0;clip:rect(0,0,0,0);clip-path:polygon(0px 0px,0px 0px,0px 0px);height:1px;margin:-1px;overflow:hidden;padding:0;position:absolute;width:1px}</style><span class="frac" role="math"><span class="num">1</span>&#8260;<span class="den">2</span></span>% on all foreign subsidies. Although there was no strong public sentiment against the practice, Pitt completely refused to profit by it. All advances were lodged by him in the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_England" title="Bank of England">Bank of England</a> until required, and all subsidies were paid over without deduction, even though it was pressed upon him, so that he did not draw a <a href="/wiki/Shilling" title="Shilling">shilling</a> from his office beyond the <a href="/wiki/Salary" title="Salary">salary</a> legally attaching to it.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Pitt ostentatiously made this clear to everyone, although he was in fact following what <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a> had done when he had held the post between 1730 and 1743. This helped to establish Pitt's reputation with the British people for honesty and placing the interests of the nation before his own.
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Broad_Bottom_ministry" title="Broad Bottom ministry">administration formed in 1746</a> lasted without major changes until 1754. It would appear from his published correspondence that Pitt had a greater influence in shaping its policy than his comparatively subordinate position would in itself have entitled him to. His support for measures, such as the Spanish Treaty and the continental subsidies, which he had violently denounced when in opposition was criticised by his enemies as an example of his political <a href="/wiki/Opportunism" title="Opportunism">opportunism</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Between 1746 and 1748 Pitt worked closely with Newcastle in formulating British military and diplomatic strategy. He shared with Newcastle a belief that Britain should continue to fight until it could receive generous peace terms, in contrast to some such as <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham" title="Henry Pelham">Henry Pelham</a> who favoured an immediate peace. Pitt was personally saddened when his friend and brother-in-law <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Grenville_(sailor)" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Grenville (sailor)">Thomas Grenville</a> was killed at the naval <a href="/wiki/First_Battle_of_Cape_Finisterre_(1747)" title="First Battle of Cape Finisterre (1747)">First Battle of Cape Finisterre</a> in 1747.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> However, this victory helped secure British supremacy of the sea which gave the British a stronger negotiating position when it came to the peace talks that ended the war. At the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Aix-la-Chapelle_(1748)" title="Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle (1748)">Treaty of Aix-la-Chapelle</a> in 1748 British colonial conquests were exchanged for a French withdrawal from <a href="/wiki/Brussels" title="Brussels">Brussels</a>. Many saw this as merely an <a href="/wiki/Armistice" title="Armistice">armistice</a> and awaited an imminent new war.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dispute_with_Newcastle">Dispute with Newcastle</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Dispute with Newcastle">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Newcastle_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="First Newcastle Ministry">First Newcastle Ministry</a></div>
<p>In 1754, Henry Pelham died suddenly, and was succeeded as Prime Minister by his brother, the Duke of Newcastle. As Newcastle sat in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>, he required a leading politician to represent the government in the House of Commons. Pitt and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Fox,_1st_Baron_Holland" title="Henry Fox, 1st Baron Holland">Henry Fox</a> were considered the two favourites for the position, but Newcastle instead rejected them both and turned to the less well-known figure of Sir <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Robinson,_1st_Baron_Grantham" title="Thomas Robinson, 1st Baron Grantham">Thomas Robinson</a>, a career <a href="/wiki/Diplomat" title="Diplomat">diplomat</a>, to fill the post. It was widely believed that Newcastle had done this because he feared the ambitions of both Pitt and Fox, and believed he would find it easier to dominate the inexperienced Robinson.<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg/170px-William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="256" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg/255px-William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg/340px-William_Pitt_discourt_au_parlement_guerre_contre_la_France.jpg 2x" data-file-width="425" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption><i>"We must declare war on France"</i>. This curious representation of William Pitt making a speech to Parliament wants to show his absolute opposition to France on colonial problems.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Despite his disappointment there was no immediate open breach. Pitt continued at his post; and at the general election which took place during the year he even accepted a nomination for the Duke's <a href="/wiki/Pocket_borough" class="mw-redirect" title="Pocket borough">pocket borough</a> of <a href="/wiki/Aldborough_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Aldborough (UK Parliament constituency)">Aldborough</a>. He had sat for <a href="/wiki/Seaford_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Seaford (UK Parliament constituency)">Seaford</a> since 1747.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The government won a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide</a>, further strengthening its majority in parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>When parliament met, however, he made no secret of his feelings. Ignoring Robinson, Pitt made frequent and vehement attacks on Newcastle himself, though still continued to serve as Paymaster under him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> From 1754 Britain was increasingly drawn into conflict with France during this period, despite Newcastle's wish to maintain the peace. The countries clashed in North America, where each had laid claim to the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_Country" title="Ohio Country">Ohio Country</a>. A British expedition under General Braddock <a href="/wiki/Braddock_Expedition" title="Braddock Expedition">had been despatched and defeated</a> in summer 1755 which caused a ratcheting up of tensions.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Henry_Fox,_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg/170px-Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="205" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg/255px-Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg/340px-Henry_Fox%2C_1st_Baron_Holland_by_John_Giles_Eccardt.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="2900" /></a><figcaption>Pitt's longstanding rival <a href="/wiki/Henry_Fox,_1st_Baron_Holland" title="Henry Fox, 1st Baron Holland">Henry Fox</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Eager to prevent the war spreading to Europe, Newcastle now tried to conclude a series of treaties that would secure Britain allies through the payment of subsidies, which he hoped would discourage France from attacking Britain. Similar subsidies had been an issue of past disagreement, and they were widely attacked by Patriot Whigs and Tories. As the government came under increasing attack, Newcastle replaced Robinson with Fox who it was acknowledged carried more political weight and again slighted Pitt.
</p><p>Finally in November 1755, Pitt was dismissed from office as paymaster, having spoken during a debate at great length against the new system of continental subsidies proposed by the government of which he was still a member.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Fox retained his own place, and though the two men continued to be of the same party, and afterwards served again in the same government, there was henceforward a rivalry between them, which makes the celebrated opposition of their sons, <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a> and <a href="/wiki/Charles_James_Fox" title="Charles James Fox">Charles James Fox</a>, seem like an inherited quarrel.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt's relationship with the Duke slumped further in early 1756 when he alleged that Newcastle was deliberately leaving the island of <a href="/wiki/Menorca" title="Menorca">Menorca</a> ill-defended so that the French would seize it, and Newcastle could use its loss to prove that Britain was not able to fight a war against France and sue for peace. When in June 1756 <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Fort_St_Philip_(1756)" title="Siege of Fort St Philip (1756)">Menorca fell</a> after <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Menorca_(1756)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Menorca (1756)">a failed attempt</a> by <a href="/wiki/John_Byng" title="John Byng">Admiral Byng</a> to relieve it, Pitt's allegations fuelled the public anger against Newcastle, leading him to be attacked by a mob in <a href="/wiki/Greenwich" title="Greenwich">Greenwich</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The loss of Menorca shattered public faith in Newcastle, and forced him to step down as Prime Minister in November 1756.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Secretary_of_State">Secretary of State</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Secretary of State">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/William_Pitt%2C_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg/220px-William_Pitt%2C_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/William_Pitt%2C_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg/330px-William_Pitt%2C_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/William_Pitt%2C_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg/440px-William_Pitt%2C_1st_Earl_of_Chatham_by_William_Hoare.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="3051" /></a><figcaption>Pitt the Elder, by <a href="/wiki/William_Hoare" title="William Hoare">William Hoare</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In December 1756, Pitt, who now sat for <a href="/wiki/Okehampton_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Okehampton (UK Parliament constituency)">Okehampton</a>, became <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_the_Southern_Department" title="Secretary of State for the Southern Department">Secretary of State for the Southern Department</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a> under the premiership of the <a href="/wiki/William_Cavendish,_4th_Duke_of_Devonshire" title="William Cavendish, 4th Duke of Devonshire">Duke of Devonshire</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> Upon entering this coalition, Pitt said to Devonshire: "My Lord, I am sure I can save this country, and no one else can".<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He had made it a condition of his joining any administration that Newcastle should be excluded from it, which proved fatal to the lengthened existence of his government. With the king unfriendly, and Newcastle, whose influence was still dominant in the Commons, estranged, it was impossible to carry on a government by the aid of public opinion alone, however emphatically that might have declared itself on his side.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> The historian <a href="/wiki/Basil_Williams_(historian)" title="Basil Williams (historian)">Basil Williams</a> has claimed that this is the first time in British history when a "man was called to supreme power by the voice of the people" rather than by the king's appointment or as the choice of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt drew up his plans for the campaigning season of 1757 in which he hoped to reverse Britain's string of defeats during the war's opening years.
</p><p>In April 1757 Pitt was dismissed from office on account of his opposition to the continental policy and the circumstances surrounding the court-martial and execution of Admiral <a href="/wiki/John_Byng" title="John Byng">John Byng</a>. He was succeeded by the Duke of Devonshire who formed the <a href="/wiki/1757_Caretaker_Ministry" class="mw-redirect" title="1757 Caretaker Ministry">1757 Caretaker Ministry</a>. But the power that was insufficient to keep him in office was strong enough to make any arrangement that excluded him impracticable. The public voice spoke in a way that was not to be mistaken. Probably no English minister ever received in so short a time so many proofs of the confidence and admiration of the public, the capital and all the chief towns voting him addresses and the freedom of their corporations<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> (e.g., London presented him with the first ever <i>honorary</i> <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_City#Freedom_of_the_City_of_London" title="Freedom of the City">Freedom</a> of the <a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City</a> awarded in history). <a href="/wiki/Horace_Walpole,_4th_Earl_of_Orford" class="mw-redirect" title="Horace Walpole, 4th Earl of Orford">Horace Walpole</a> recorded the freedoms of various cities awarded to Pitt:
</p>
<blockquote><p>... for some weeks it rained gold boxes: <a href="/wiki/Chester" title="Chester">Chester</a>, <a href="/wiki/Worcester,_England" title="Worcester, England">Worcester</a>, <a href="/wiki/Norwich" title="Norwich">Norwich</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bedford" title="Bedford">Bedford</a>, <a href="/wiki/Salisbury" title="Salisbury">Salisbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Great_Yarmouth" title="Great Yarmouth">Yarmouth</a>, <a href="/wiki/Tewkesbury" title="Tewkesbury">Tewkesbury</a>, <a href="/wiki/Newcastle-on-Tyne" class="mw-redirect" title="Newcastle-on-Tyne">Newcastle-on-Tyne</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stirling" title="Stirling">Stirling</a>, and other populous and chief towns following the example. <a href="/wiki/Exeter" title="Exeter">Exeter</a>, with singular affection, sent boxes of oak.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>After some weeks' negotiation, in the course of which the firmness and moderation of "<i>The Great Commoner</i>", as he had come to be called, contrasted favourably with the characteristic tortuosities of the crafty <a href="/wiki/Peerage" title="Peerage">peer</a>, matters were settled on such a basis that, while Newcastle was the nominal, Pitt was the virtual head of the government. On his acceptance of office, he was chosen member for <a href="/wiki/Bath_(UK_Parliament_constituency)" title="Bath (UK Parliament constituency)">Bath</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Pitt.E2.80.93Newcastle_ministry"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Pitt–Newcastle_ministry">Pitt–Newcastle ministry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Pitt–Newcastle ministry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Pitt%E2%80%93Newcastle_ministry" title="Pitt–Newcastle ministry">Pitt–Newcastle ministry</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Great_Britain_in_the_Seven_Years_War" class="mw-redirect" title="Great Britain in the Seven Years War">Great Britain in the Seven Years War</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Thomas_Pelham-Holles_(1693-1768).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Thomas_Pelham-Holles_%281693-1768%29.jpg/220px-Thomas_Pelham-Holles_%281693-1768%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="255" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/22/Thomas_Pelham-Holles_%281693-1768%29.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="305" data-file-height="354" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Pelham-Holles,_1st_Duke_of_Newcastle-upon-Tyne" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Pelham-Holles, 1st Duke of Newcastle-upon-Tyne">Duke of Newcastle</a> with whom Pitt formed an unlikely political partnership from 1757</figcaption></figure>
<p>A <a href="/wiki/Pitt%E2%80%93Newcastle_ministry" title="Pitt–Newcastle ministry">coalition with Newcastle</a> was formed in June 1757, and held power until October 1761. It brought together several various factions and was built around the partnership between Pitt and Newcastle, which a few months earlier had seemed impossible. The two men used <a href="/wiki/Philip_Stanhope,_4th_Earl_of_Chesterfield" title="Philip Stanhope, 4th Earl of Chesterfield">Lord Chesterfield</a> as an intermediary and had managed to agree a division of powers that was acceptable to both.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> For the past few months Britain had been virtually leaderless, although Devonshire had remained formally Prime Minister, but now Pitt and Newcastle were ready to offer stronger direction to the country's strategy.
</p><p>By summer 1757 the British war effort over the previous three years had broadly been a failure. Britain's attempts to take the offensive in North America had ended in disaster, Menorca had been lost, and the <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Cumberland" title="Duke of Cumberland">Duke of Cumberland</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Army_of_Observation" class="mw-redirect" title="Army of Observation">Army of Observation</a> was retreating across <a href="/wiki/Hanover" title="Hanover">Hanover</a> following the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Hastenback" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Hastenback">Battle of Hastenback</a>. In October Cumberland was forced to conclude the <a href="/wiki/Convention_of_Klosterzeven" title="Convention of Klosterzeven">Convention of Klosterzeven</a>, which would take Hanover out of the war.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Hanover_(1757)" title="Invasion of Hanover (1757)">French Invasion of Hanover</a> posed a threat to Britain's ally Prussia, which was now vulnerable to attack from the west by the French as well as facing attack from Austria, <a href="/wiki/Russian_Empire" title="Russian Empire">Russia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Electorate_of_Saxony" title="Electorate of Saxony">Saxony</a> and Sweden.
</p><p>Although it was late in the campaigning season when he had come to power, Pitt set about trying to initiate a more assertive strategy. He conspired with a number of figures to persuade the Hanoverians to revoke the Klosterzevern Convention and re-enter the war on Britain's side, which they did in late 1757. He also put into practice a scheme of <a href="/wiki/Naval_Descents" class="mw-redirect" title="Naval Descents">Naval Descents</a> that would make <a href="/wiki/Amphibious_warfare" title="Amphibious warfare">amphibious</a> landings on the French coast. The first of these, the <a href="/wiki/Raid_on_Rochefort" title="Raid on Rochefort">Raid on Rochefort</a>, took place in September but was not a success.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> The centrepiece of the campaign in North America, an <a href="/wiki/Louisbourg_Expedition_(1757)" title="Louisbourg Expedition (1757)">expedition to capture Louisbourg</a>, was aborted due to the presence of a large French fleet and a gale that scattered the British fleet.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1758">1758</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: 1758">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1758 Pitt began to put into practice a new strategy to win the Seven Years' War, which would involve tying down large numbers of French troops and resources in Germany, while Britain used its naval supremacy to launch expeditions to capture French forces around the globe. Following the capture of Emden he ordered the dispatch of the first British troops to the European continent under the <a href="/wiki/Charles_Spencer,_3rd_Duke_of_Marlborough" title="Charles Spencer, 3rd Duke of Marlborough">Duke of Marlborough</a>, who joined <a href="/wiki/Duke_Ferdinand_of_Brunswick" class="mw-redirect" title="Duke Ferdinand of Brunswick">Brunswick</a>'s army.<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> This was a dramatic reversal of his previous position, as he had recently been strongly opposed to any such commitment.
</p><p>Pitt had been lobbied by an American merchant <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Cumming" title="Thomas Cumming">Thomas Cumming</a> to launch an expedition against the French trading settlements in <a href="/wiki/West_Africa" title="West Africa">West Africa</a>. In April 1758 <a href="/wiki/Capture_of_Senegal" class="mw-redirect" title="Capture of Senegal">British forces captured</a> the ill-defended fort of <a href="/wiki/Saint-Louis,_Senegal" title="Saint-Louis, Senegal">Saint-Louis</a> in <a href="/wiki/Senegal" title="Senegal">Senegal</a>. The mission was so lucrative that Pitt sent out further expeditions to capture <a href="/wiki/Capture_of_Gor%C3%A9e" title="Capture of Gorée">Gorée</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gambia" class="mw-redirect" title="Gambia">Gambia</a> later in the year.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> He also drew up plans to attack French islands in the <a href="/wiki/Caribbean" title="Caribbean">Caribbean</a> the following year at the suggestion of a <a href="/wiki/Jamaica" title="Jamaica">Jamaican</a> sugar planter <a href="/wiki/William_Beckford_(politician)" title="William Beckford (politician)">William Beckford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In North America, a <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Louisbourg_(1758)" title="Siege of Louisbourg (1758)">second British attempt</a> to capture <a href="/wiki/Louisbourg" title="Louisbourg">Louisbourg</a> succeeded. However, Pitt's pleasure over this was tempered by the subsequent news of a significant British defeat at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Carillon" title="Battle of Carillon">Battle of Carillon</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> Toward the end of the year the <a href="/wiki/Forbes_Expedition" title="Forbes Expedition">Forbes Expedition</a> seized the site of <a href="/wiki/Fort_Duquesne" title="Fort Duquesne">Fort Duquesne</a> and began constructing the British-controlled Fort Pitt (which later grew into the city of Pittsburgh). This gave the British control of the <a href="/wiki/Ohio_Country" title="Ohio Country">Ohio Country</a>, which had been the principal cause of the war.
</p><p>In Europe, Brunswick's forces enjoyed a mixed year. Brunswick had crossed the <a href="/wiki/Rhine" title="Rhine">Rhine</a>, but faced with being cut off he had retreated and blocked any potential French move towards Hanover with his victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Krefeld" title="Battle of Krefeld">Battle of Krefeld</a>. The year ended with something approaching a stalemate in Germany. Pitt had continued his naval descents during 1758, but the first had enjoyed only limited success and the second ended with near disaster at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_St_Cast" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of St Cast">Battle of St Cast</a> and no further descents were planned.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> Instead the troops and ships would be used as part of the coming expedition to the <a href="/wiki/French_West_Indies" title="French West Indies">French West Indies</a>. The scheme of amphibious raids was the only one of Pitt's policies during the war that was broadly a failure, although it did help briefly relieve pressure on the German front by tying down French troops on coastal protection service.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Annus_Mirabilis">Annus Mirabilis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Annus Mirabilis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Annus_Mirabilis_of_1759" class="mw-redirect" title="Annus Mirabilis of 1759">Annus Mirabilis of 1759</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg/250px-PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="161" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg/375px-PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg/500px-PlainsOfAbraham2007.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2610" data-file-height="1683" /></a><figcaption>James Wolfe's victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Quebec_(1759)" class="mw-redirect" title="Battle of Quebec (1759)">Battle of Quebec</a> in 1759</figcaption></figure>
<p>In France a new leader, the <a href="/wiki/%C3%89tienne_Fran%C3%A7ois,_duc_de_Choiseul" class="mw-redirect" title="Étienne François, duc de Choiseul">Duc de Choiseul</a>, had recently come to power and 1759 offered a duel between their rival strategies. Pitt intended to continue with his plan of tying down French forces in Germany while continuing the assault on France's colonies. Choiseul hoped to repel the attacks in the colonies while seeking total victory in Europe.
</p><p>Pitt's war around the world was largely successful. While a British <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Martinique_(1759)" title="Invasion of Martinique (1759)">invasion of Martinique</a> failed, they <a href="/wiki/Invasion_of_Guadeloupe_(1759)" title="Invasion of Guadeloupe (1759)">captured Guadeloupe</a> shortly afterwards. In India, a French <a href="/wiki/Siege_of_Madras" title="Siege of Madras">attempt to capture Madras</a> was repulsed. In North America, British troops closed in on France's Canadian heartland. A British force under <a href="/wiki/James_Wolfe" title="James Wolfe">James Wolfe</a> moved up the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Lawrence_River" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Lawrence River">Saint Lawrence</a> with the aim of capturing <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>. After initially <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Beauport" title="Battle of Beauport">failing to penetrate</a> the French defences at the <a href="/wiki/Montmorency_Falls" title="Montmorency Falls">Montmorency Falls</a>, Wolfe later led his men <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_the_Plains_of_Abraham" title="Battle of the Plains of Abraham">to a victory</a> to the west of the city allowing the British forces to capture Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Choiseul had pinned much of his hopes on <a href="/wiki/Planned_French_Invasion_of_Britain_(1759)" class="mw-redirect" title="Planned French Invasion of Britain (1759)">a French invasion of Britain</a>, which he hoped would knock Britain out of the war and make it surrender the colonies it had taken from France. Pitt had stripped the home islands of troops to send on his expeditions, leaving Britain guarded by poorly trained militia and giving an opportunity for the French if they could land in enough force. The French did build a large invasion force. However the French naval defeats at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lagos" title="Battle of Lagos">Lagos</a> and <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Quiberon_Bay" title="Battle of Quiberon Bay">Quiberon Bay</a> forced Choiseul to abandon the invasion plans. France's other great hope, that their armies could make a breakthrough in Germany and invade Hanover, was thwarted at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Minden" title="Battle of Minden">Battle of Minden</a>. Britain ended the year victorious in every theatre of operations in which it was engaged, with Pitt receiving the credit for this.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span id="1760.E2.80.9361"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="1760–61">1760–61</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: 1760–61">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Britain completed the <a href="/wiki/Conquest_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Conquest of Canada">conquest of Canada</a> in 1760 by capturing Montreal, which effectively brought the war to an end on mainland North America.
</p><p>Pitt's power had now reached its peak, but was soon under threat. The domestic political situation was altered dramatically when George II died in October 1760. He was succeeded by his grandson, <a href="/wiki/George_III" title="George III">George III</a>, who had once considered Pitt an ally but had become angered by Pitt's alliance with Newcastle and acceptance of the need for British intervention in Germany&#160;&#8211;&#32;which George was strongly opposed to.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> The new king successfully lobbied for his favourite <a href="/wiki/John_Stuart,_3rd_Earl_of_Bute" title="John Stuart, 3rd Earl of Bute">Lord Bute</a> to be given the post of <a href="/wiki/Northern_Secretary" class="mw-redirect" title="Northern Secretary">Northern Secretary</a>. Bute was inclined to support a withdrawal from Germany, and to fight the war with France largely at sea and in the colonies.
</p><p>Pitt's plan for an expedition to capture <a href="/wiki/Belle_%C3%8Ele" title="Belle Île">Belle Île</a> was put into force in April 1761 and it was <a href="/wiki/Capture_of_Belle_%C3%8Ele" title="Capture of Belle Île">captured after a siege</a>. This provided yet a further blow to French prestige, as it was the first part of <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_France" title="Metropolitan France">Metropolitan France</a> to be occupied.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Pitt now expected France to offer terms, although he was prepared for a longer war if necessary. Envoys were exchanged, but neither side could reach an agreement.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> Pitt's refusal to grant the French a share in <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland_(island)" title="Newfoundland (island)">Newfoundland</a> proved the biggest obstacle to peace, as Pitt declared he would rather lose the use of his right arm than give the French a share there and later said he would rather give up the <a href="/wiki/Tower_of_London" title="Tower of London">Tower of London</a> than Newfoundland. Newfoundland was at the time seen as possessing huge economic and strategic value because of the extensive fishing industry there.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The war in Germany continued through 1761 with the French again attempting to overcome Brunswick and invade Hanover, but suffering a defeat at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Villinghausen" title="Battle of Villinghausen">Battle of Villinghausen</a>. Pitt had substantially increased the number of British troops serving with Brunswick, and he also planned further conquests in the West Indies. A strategy he hoped would compel the French to conclude a reasonable peace treaty.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Treaty_of_Paris">Treaty of Paris</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Treaty of Paris">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1763)" title="Treaty of Paris (1763)">Treaty of Paris (1763)</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:NorthAmerica1762-83.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/NorthAmerica1762-83.png/250px-NorthAmerica1762-83.png" decoding="async" width="250" height="191" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/NorthAmerica1762-83.png/375px-NorthAmerica1762-83.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/NorthAmerica1762-83.png/500px-NorthAmerica1762-83.png 2x" data-file-width="787" data-file-height="600" /></a><figcaption>New borders drawn by the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Proclamation_of_1763" title="Royal Proclamation of 1763">Royal Proclamation of 1763</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>To the preliminaries of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1763)" title="Treaty of Paris (1763)">peace</a> concluded in February 1763 he offered an indignant resistance, considering the terms quite inadequate to the successes that had been gained by the country. When the treaty was discussed in parliament in December of the previous year, though suffering from a severe attack of gout, he was carried down to the House, and in a speech of three hours' duration, interrupted more than once by paroxysms of pain, he strongly protested against its various conditions.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> These conditions included the return of the sugar islands (but Britain retained <a href="/wiki/Dominica" title="Dominica">Dominica</a>); trading stations in West Africa (won by Boscawen); <a href="/wiki/Pondicherry_(city)" class="mw-redirect" title="Pondicherry (city)">Pondicherry</a> (<a href="/wiki/French_India" title="French India">France's Indian colony</a>); and fishing rights in <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland" class="mw-redirect" title="Newfoundland">Newfoundland</a>.
