Pitt's opposition arose through two heads: France had been given the means to become once more formidable at sea, whilst <a href="/wiki/Frederick_the_Great" title="Frederick the Great">Frederick of Prussia</a> had been betrayed.
</p><p>Pitt believed that the task had been left half-finished and called for a final year of war which would crush French power for good. Pitt had long-held plans for further conquests which had been uncompleted. Newcastle, by contrast, sought peace but only if the war in Germany could be brought to an honourable and satisfactory conclusion (rather than Britain suddenly bailing out of it as Bute proposed). However the combined opposition of Newcastle and Pitt was not enough to prevent the Treaty passing comfortably in both Houses of Parliament.
</p><p>However, there were strong reasons for concluding the peace: the National Debt had increased from £74.5m. in 1755 to £133.25m. in 1763, the year of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1763)" title="Treaty of Paris (1763)">peace</a>. The requirement to pay down this debt, and the lack of French threat in Canada, were major movers in the subsequent <a href="/wiki/American_War_of_Independence" class="mw-redirect" title="American War of Independence">American War of Independence</a>.
</p><p>The physical cause which rendered this effort so painful probably accounts for the infrequency of his appearances in parliament, as well as for much that is otherwise inexplicable in his subsequent conduct. In 1763 he spoke against the unpopular <a href="/wiki/Cider_Bill_of_1763" title="Cider Bill of 1763">tax on cider</a>, imposed by his brother-in-law, <a href="/wiki/George_Grenville" title="George Grenville">George Grenville</a>, and his opposition, though unsuccessful in the House, helped to keep alive his popularity with the country, which cordially hated the <a href="/wiki/Excise" title="Excise">excise</a> and all connected with it. When next year the question of <a href="/wiki/General_warrant" class="mw-redirect" title="General warrant">general warrants</a> was raised in connexion with the case of <a href="/wiki/John_Wilkes" title="John Wilkes">John Wilkes</a>, Pitt vigorously maintained their illegality, thus defending at once the privileges of Parliament and the <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press" title="Freedom of the press">freedom of the press</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>During 1765 he seems to have been totally <a href="https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/incapacitation" class="extiw" title="wikt:incapacitation">incapacitated</a> for public business. In the following year he supported with great power the proposal of the <a href="/wiki/Charles_Watson-Wentworth,_2nd_Marquess_of_Rockingham" title="Charles Watson-Wentworth, 2nd Marquess of Rockingham">Rockingham</a> administration for the repeal of the  <a href="/wiki/Stamp_Act_1765" title="Stamp Act 1765">Stamp Act</a>, arguing that it was unconstitutional to impose taxes upon the colonies. He thus endorsed the contention of the colonists on the ground of principle, while the majority of those who acted with him contented themselves with resisting the disastrous taxation scheme on the ground of expediency.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1134653256">.mw-parser-output .side-box{margin:4px 0;box-sizing:border-box;border:1px solid #aaa;font-size:88%;line-height:1.25em;background-color:#f9f9f9;display:flow-root}.mw-parser-output .side-box-abovebelow,.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{padding:0.25em 0.9em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-image{padding:2px 0 2px 0.9em;text-align:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-imageright{padding:2px 0.9em 2px 0;text-align:center}@media(min-width:500px){.mw-parser-output .side-box-flex{display:flex;align-items:center}.mw-parser-output .side-box-text{flex:1}}@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .side-box{width:238px}.mw-parser-output .side-box-right{clear:right;float:right;margin-left:1em}.mw-parser-output .side-box-left{margin-right:1em}}</style><div class="side-box side-box-right plainlinks sistersitebox"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409">
<div class="side-box-flex">
<div class="side-box-image"><span class="noviewer" typeof="mw:File"><span><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/38px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png" decoding="async" width="38" height="40" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/57px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Wikisource-logo.svg/76px-Wikisource-logo.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="410" data-file-height="430" /></span></span></div>
<div class="side-box-text plainlist"><a href="/wiki/Wikisource" title="Wikisource">Wikisource</a> has original text related to this article:
<div style="margin-left: 10px;"><b><a href="https://en.wikisource.org/wiki/Speech_against_the_Stamp_Act" class="extiw" title="wikisource:Speech against the Stamp Act">Speech against the Stamp Act</a></b></div></div></div>
</div>
<p>The Repeal (1766) of the Stamp Act, indeed, was only passed <i><a href="/wiki/Pari_passu" title="Pari passu">pari passu</a></i> with <a href="/wiki/Declaratory_Act" title="Declaratory Act">another</a> censuring the American <a href="/wiki/Deliberative_assembly" title="Deliberative assembly">assemblies</a>, and declaring the authority of the British parliament over the colonies "in all cases whatsoever". Thus the House of Commons repudiated in the most formal manner the principle Pitt laid down. His language in approval of the resistance of the colonists was unusually bold, and perhaps no one but himself could have employed it with impunity at a time when the freedom of debate was only imperfectly conceded.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt had not been long out of office when he was solicited to return to it, and the solicitations were more than once renewed. Unsuccessful overtures were made to him in 1763, and twice in 1765, in May and June—the negotiator in May being the king's uncle, the <a href="/wiki/Prince_William_Augustus,_Duke_of_Cumberland" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince William Augustus, Duke of Cumberland">Duke of Cumberland</a>, who went down in person to <a href="/wiki/Hayes,_Bromley" title="Hayes, Bromley">Hayes</a>, Pitt's seat in <a href="/wiki/Kent" title="Kent">Kent</a>. It is known that he had the opportunity of joining the Marquess of Rockingham's short-lived administration at any time on his own terms, and his conduct in declining an arrangement with that minister has been more generally condemned than any other step in his public life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Leadership">Leadership</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Leadership">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The <i>London Magazine</i> of 1767 offered "Pitt, Pompadour, Prussia, Providence" as the reasons for Britain's success in the <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> Pitt's relation to all three was such as to entitle him to a large share in the credit of their deeds. He inspired trust in his chosen commanders by his indifference to rules of seniority—several of "Pitt's boys", like <a href="/wiki/Augustus_Keppel,_1st_Viscount_Keppel" title="Augustus Keppel, 1st Viscount Keppel">Keppel</a>, captor of <a href="/wiki/Gor%C3%A9e" title="Gorée">Gorée</a>, were in their thirties—and by his clear orders.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> It was his discernment that selected Wolfe to lead the attack on <a href="/wiki/Quebec" title="Quebec">Quebec</a>, and gave him the opportunity of dying a victor on the <a href="/wiki/Plains_of_Abraham" title="Plains of Abraham">heights of Abraham</a>. He had personally less to do with the successes in India than with the other great enterprises that shed an undying lustre on his administration; but his generous praise in parliament encouraged <a href="/wiki/Robert_Clive" title="Robert Clive">Robert Clive</a>, and the forces that acted at the close of the struggle were animated by his indomitable spirit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Clive.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Clive.jpg/220px-Clive.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="171" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Clive.jpg/330px-Clive.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4f/Clive.jpg/440px-Clive.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="1870" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Robert_Clive" title="Robert Clive">Robert Clive</a>'s victory at the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Plassey" title="Battle of Plassey">Battle of Plassey</a> established the <a href="/wiki/East_India_Company" title="East India Company">East India Company</a> as a military as well as a commercial power.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pitt's particular genius was to finance an army on the continent to drain French men and resources so that Britain might concentrate on what he held to be the vital spheres: Canada and the <a href="/wiki/West_Indies" title="West Indies">West Indies</a>; whilst Clive successfully defeated <a href="/wiki/Siraj_ud-Daulah" title="Siraj ud-Daulah">Siraj ud-Daulah</a>, (the last independent <a href="/wiki/Nawab_of_Bengal" class="mw-redirect" title="Nawab of Bengal">Nawab of Bengal</a>) at <a href="/wiki/Plassey" class="mw-redirect" title="Plassey">Plassey</a> (1757), securing India. The Continental campaign was carried on by <a href="/wiki/Prince_William_Augustus,_Duke_of_Cumberland" class="mw-redirect" title="Prince William Augustus, Duke of Cumberland">Cumberland</a>, defeated at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Hastenbeck" title="Battle of Hastenbeck">Hastenbeck</a> and forced to surrender at <a href="/wiki/Convention_of_Klosterzeven" title="Convention of Klosterzeven">Convention of Klosterzeven</a> (1757) and thereafter by <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_of_Brunswick" class="mw-redirect" title="Ferdinand of Brunswick">Ferdinand of Brunswick</a>, later victor at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Minden" title="Battle of Minden">Minden</a>; Britain's Continental campaign had two major strands, firstly subsidising allies, particularly Frederick the Great, and second, financing an army to divert French resources from the colonial war and to also defend Hanover (which was the territory of the Kings of England at this time)
</p><p>Pitt, was a leading Imperialist in English history. He was the directing mind in the expansion of his country, and with him the beginning of empire is rightly associated. The <a href="/wiki/Seven_Years%27_War" title="Seven Years&#39; War">Seven Years' War</a> might well, moreover, have been another <a href="/wiki/Thirty_Years%27_War" title="Thirty Years&#39; War">Thirty Years' War</a> if Pitt had not furnished Frederick with an annual subsidy of £700,000, and in addition relieved him of the task of defending western Germany against France:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> this was the policy that allowed Pitt to boast of having "won Canada on the banks of the Rhine".
</p><p>Contemporary opinion was, of course, incompetent to estimate the permanent results gained for the country by the brilliant foreign policy of Pitt. It has long been generally agreed that by several of his most costly expeditions nothing was really won but glory: the policy of diversionary attacks on places like <a href="/wiki/Rochefort,_Charente-Maritime" title="Rochefort, Charente-Maritime">Rochefort</a> was memorably described as 'breaking windows with gold guineas'. It has even been said that the only permanent acquisition that England owed directly to him was her Canadian dominion; and, strictly speaking, this is true, it being admitted that the campaign by which the Indian empire was virtually won was not planned by him, though brought to a successful issue during his ministry.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>But material <i>aggrandisement</i>, though the only tangible, was not the only real or lasting effect of a war policy. More could be gained by crushing a formidable rival than by conquering a province. The loss of her Canadian possessions was only one of a series of disasters suffered by France, which included the victories at sea of <a href="/wiki/Edward_Boscawen" title="Edward Boscawen">Boscawen</a> at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Lagos" title="Battle of Lagos">Lagos</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edward_Hawke" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward Hawke">Hawke</a> at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Quiberon_Bay" title="Battle of Quiberon Bay">Quiberon Bay</a>. Such defeats radically affected the future of Europe and the world. Deprived of her most valuable colonies both in the <a href="/wiki/Asia" title="Asia">East</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/North_America" title="North America">West</a>, and thoroughly defeated on the continent, France's humiliation was the beginning of a new epoch in history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The victorious policy of Pitt destroyed the military prestige which repeated experience has shown to be in France as in no other country the very life of monarchy, and thus was not the least of the influences that slowly brought about the <a href="/wiki/French_Revolution" title="French Revolution">French Revolution</a>. It effectually deprived France of the lead in the councils of Europe which she had hitherto arrogated to herself, and so affected the whole course of continental politics. It is such far-reaching results as these, and not the mere acquisition of a single colony, however valuable, that constitute Pitt's claim to be considered as the most powerful minister that ever guided the foreign policy of England.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113_40-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19113-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Resignation">Resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Resignation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:JohnStuartBute.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/JohnStuartBute.jpg/220px-JohnStuartBute.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="220" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/c/ce/JohnStuartBute.jpg 1.5x" data-file-width="274" data-file-height="274" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/John_Stuart,_3rd_Earl_of_Bute" title="John Stuart, 3rd Earl of Bute">Lord Bute</a>'s rise to power between 1760 and 1762 dramatically influenced the emphasis of Britain's war effort. Like the new king, Bute favoured an end to British involvement on the continent.</figcaption></figure>
<p>George II died on 25 October 1760, and was succeeded by his grandson, <a href="/wiki/George_III_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="George III of the United Kingdom">George III</a>. The new king was inclined to view politics in personal terms and taught to believe that "Pitt had the blackest of hearts". The new king had counsellors of his own, led by <a href="/wiki/John_Stuart,_3rd_Earl_of_Bute" title="John Stuart, 3rd Earl of Bute">Lord Bute</a>. Bute soon joined the cabinet as a <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_state" title="Secretary of state">Northern Secretary</a> and Pitt and he were quickly in dispute over a number of issues.
</p><p>In 1761 Pitt had received information from his agents about a secret <a href="/wiki/Pacte_de_Famille" title="Pacte de Famille">Bourbon Family Compact</a> by which the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Bourbon" title="House of Bourbon">Bourbons</a> of France and Spain bound themselves in an offensive alliance against Britain.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Spain was concerned that Britain's victories over France had left them too powerful, and were a threat in the long term to Spain's <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">own empire</a>. Equally they may have believed that the British had become overstretched by fighting a global war and decided to try to seize British possessions such as <a href="/wiki/Jamaica" title="Jamaica">Jamaica</a>. A secret convention pledged that if Britain and France were still at war by 1 May 1762, Spain would enter the war on the French side.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt urged that such a clear threat should be met by a pre-emptive strike against Spain's navy and her colonies—with emphasis on speed to prevent Spain bringing the annual <a href="/wiki/Manila_galleon" title="Manila galleon">Manila galleon</a> safely to harbour. Bute and Newcastle refused to support such a move, as did the entire cabinet except <a href="/wiki/Richard_Grenville-Temple,_2nd_Earl_Temple" title="Richard Grenville-Temple, 2nd Earl Temple">Temple</a>, believing it would make Britain look the aggressor against Spain potentially provoking other neutral nations to declare war on Britain. Pitt believed he had no choice but to leave a cabinet in which his advice on a vital question had been rejected and presented his resignation. Many of his cabinet colleagues secretly welcomed his departure as they believed his dominance and popularity were a threat to the Constitution.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup> Pitt's brother-in-law <a href="/wiki/George_Grenville" title="George Grenville">George Grenville</a> was given a major role in government, angering Pitt who felt Grenville should have resigned with him. Pitt regarded Grenville's action as a betrayal and there was hostility between them for several years.
</p><p>After Pitt's resignation in October 1761, the King urged Pitt to accept a mark of royal favour. Accordingly, he obtained a pension of £3000 a year and his wife, <a href="/wiki/Hester_Pitt,_Countess_of_Chatham" title="Hester Pitt, Countess of Chatham">Lady Hester Grenville</a> was created <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Chatham" title="Earl of Chatham">Baroness Chatham</a> in her own right—although Pitt refused to accept a title himself. Pitt assured the King that he would not go into direct opposition against the government. His conduct after his retirement was distinguished by a moderation and disinterestedness which, as <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Burke" title="Edmund Burke">Edmund Burke</a> remarked, "set a seal upon his character". The war with Spain, in which he had urged the cabinet to take the initiative, proved inevitable; but he scorned to use the occasion for "altercation and recrimination", and spoke in support of the government measures for carrying on the war.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Twenty years after he had received a similar windfall from the Marlborough legacy, <a href="/wiki/Sir_William_Pynsent,_2nd_Baronet" title="Sir William Pynsent, 2nd Baronet">Sir William Pynsent, Bt.</a>, a <a href="/wiki/Somerset" title="Somerset">Somerset</a> <a href="/wiki/Baronet" title="Baronet">baronet</a> to whom he was personally quite unknown, left him his entire estate, worth about three thousand a year, in testimony of approval of his political career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112_18-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19112-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Chatham_Ministry">The Chatham Ministry<span class="anchor" id="Premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: The Chatham Ministry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Chatham_ministry" title="Chatham ministry">Chatham ministry</a></div>
<p>In July 1766 Rockingham was dismissed, and Pitt was entrusted by the King with the task of forming a government entirely of his own selection.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> His principle, "measures not men", appealed to the King whom he proposed to serve by "destroying all party distinctions". Pitt made appointments based not on connections but on merit, such as <a href="/wiki/Charles_Townshend" title="Charles Townshend">Charles Townshend</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Exchequer" title="Exchequer">Exchequer</a> and <a href="/wiki/William_Petty,_2nd_Earl_of_Shelburne" title="William Petty, 2nd Earl of Shelburne">Shelburne</a> as Secretary of State, to order American affairs. Pitt chose for himself the office of <a href="/wiki/Lord_Privy_Seal" title="Lord Privy Seal">Lord Privy Seal</a>, which required his elevation to the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Lords" title="House of Lords">House of Lords</a>, and on 4 August he became <b>Earl of <a href="/wiki/Chatham,_Kent" title="Chatham, Kent">Chatham</a> in the county of Kent</b> and <b>Viscount Pitt of <a href="/wiki/Burton_Pynsent" class="mw-redirect" title="Burton Pynsent">Burton Pynsent</a> in the county of Somerset</b>.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pitt's decision to accept a <a href="/wiki/Peerage" title="Peerage">peerage</a> was likely influenced by his declining health and desire for a less demanding role, but the "great commoner" lost a great deal of public support.<sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup> For example, in view of his probable accession to power, preparations were made in the <a href="/wiki/City_of_London" title="City of London">City of London</a> for a banquet and a general illumination to celebrate the event, but the celebration was at once countermanded when it was known that he had become Earl of Chatham.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Edmund_Burke" title="Edmund Burke">Edmund Burke</a> described the administration as "chequered and speckled", and spoke of it as "patriots and courtiers, King's friends and republicans; Whigs and Tories&#160; ... indeed a very curious show, but utterly unsafe to touch and unsure to stand on".<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The problems facing the government included the observance of the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Paris_(1763)" title="Treaty of Paris (1763)">Treaty of Paris</a> by France and Spain, tension between American colonists and the mother country, and the status of the <a href="/wiki/British_East_India_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="British East India Company">East India Company</a>. One of the new ministry's earliest acts was to lay an <a href="/wiki/Embargo" class="mw-redirect" title="Embargo">embargo</a> upon <a href="/wiki/Cereal" title="Cereal">corn</a>, which was thought necessary in order to prevent a dearth resulting from the unprecedented bad harvest of 1766. The measure was strongly opposed, and Lord Chatham delivered his first speech in the House of Lords in support of it. It proved to be almost the only measure introduced by his government in which he personally interested himself.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1767, Charles Townshend, the Chancellor of the Exchequer, enacted <a href="/wiki/Townshend_Acts" title="Townshend Acts">duties</a> in the American colonies on tea, paper, and other goods. The taxes were created without the consultation of Chatham and possibly against his wishes. They proved offensive to the American colonists. Chatham's attention had been directed to the growing importance of the affairs of India, and there is evidence in his correspondence that he was meditating a comprehensive scheme for transferring much of the power of the <a href="/wiki/British_East_India_Company" class="mw-redirect" title="British East India Company">East India Company</a> to the crown. Yet he was incapacitated physically and mentally during nearly his entire tenure of office.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> Chatham rarely saw any of his colleagues though they repeatedly and urgently pressed for interviews with him, and even an offer from the King to visit him in person was respectfully declined. While his gout seemed to improve, he was newly afflicted with <a href="/wiki/Mental_illness" class="mw-redirect" title="Mental illness">mental alienation</a> bordering on <a href="/wiki/Insanity" title="Insanity">insanity</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114_62-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19114-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> Chatham's lack of leadership resulted in an incohesive set of policies.
</p><p>Chatham dismissed his allies <a href="/wiki/Jeffrey_Amherst,_1st_Baron_Amherst" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeffrey Amherst, 1st Baron Amherst">Amherst</a> and Shelburne from their posts, and then in October 1768 he tendered his own resignation on the grounds of poor health, leaving the leadership to <a href="/wiki/Augustus_FitzRoy,_3rd_Duke_of_Grafton" title="Augustus FitzRoy, 3rd Duke of Grafton">Grafton</a>, his <a href="/wiki/First_Lord_of_the_Treasury" title="First Lord of the Treasury">First Lord of the Treasury</a>.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Later_life">Later life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Later life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png/220px-Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="132" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png/330px-Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png/440px-Pitt_family_coat_of_arms.png 2x" data-file-width="1794" data-file-height="1074" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Coat_of_arms" title="Coat of arms">Coat of arms</a> of William Pitt.</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pitt_arms.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Pitt_arms.svg/220px-Pitt_arms.svg.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="242" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Pitt_arms.svg/330px-Pitt_arms.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d7/Pitt_arms.svg/440px-Pitt_arms.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="512" data-file-height="563" /></a><figcaption>Arms of William Pitt. Note that his arms form the basis for those of <a href="/wiki/Seal_of_Pittsburgh,_Pennsylvania" class="mw-redirect" title="Seal of Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania">Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania</a> and the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Pittsburgh" title="University of Pittsburgh">University of Pittsburgh</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Soon after his resignation a renewed attack of gout freed Chatham from the mental disease under which he had so long suffered. He had been nearly two years and a half in seclusion when, in July 1769, he again appeared in public at a royal levee. It was not, however, until 1770 that he resumed his seat in the House of Lords.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Falklands_Crisis">Falklands Crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Falklands Crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Falklands_Crisis_(1770)" class="mw-redirect" title="Falklands Crisis (1770)">Falklands Crisis (1770)</a></div>
<p>The same year when Britain and Spain became involved in the <a href="/wiki/Falklands_Crisis_(1770)" class="mw-redirect" title="Falklands Crisis (1770)">Falklands Crisis</a> and came close to war, Pitt was a staunch advocate of taking a tough stance with Madrid and Paris (as he had been during the earlier <a href="/wiki/Corsican_Crisis" title="Corsican Crisis">Corsican Crisis</a> when France <a href="/wiki/French_conquest_of_Corsica" title="French conquest of Corsica">had invaded Corsica</a>) and made a number of speeches on the subject rousing public opinion.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup> The government of <a href="/wiki/Frederick_North,_Lord_North" title="Frederick North, Lord North">Lord North</a> was pushed into taking a firmer line because of this, mobilising the navy, and forcing Spain to back down. Some had even believed that the issue was enough to cast North from office and restore Pitt as Prime Minister—although the ultimate result was to strengthen the position of North who took credit for his firm handling of the crisis and was able to fill the cabinet with his own supporters. North would go on to dominate politics for the next decade, leading the country until 1782.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="War_of_American_Independence">War of American Independence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: War of American Independence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/War_of_American_Independence" class="mw-redirect" title="War of American Independence">War of American Independence</a></div>
<p>Chatham sought to find a compromise on the escalating conflict with the American colonies.  As he realised the gravity of the American situation, Chatham re-entered the fray, declaring that he would "be in earnest for the public" and "a scarecrow of violence to the gentler warblers of the grove".<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> His position changed from an obsession in 1774 with the question of the authority of Parliament to a search for a formula for conciliation in 1775. He proposed the "Provisional Act" that would both maintain the ultimate authority of Parliamentary sovereignty, while meeting the colonial demands. The Lords defeated his proposal on 1 February 1775.  Chatham's warnings regarding America were ignored. His brave efforts to present his case, passionate, deeply pondered, for the concession of fundamental liberties—no taxation without consent, independent judges, trial by jury, along with the recognition of the American <a href="/wiki/Continental_Congress" title="Continental Congress">Continental Congress</a>—foundered on the arrogance and complacency of his peers.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
After war had broken out, he warned that <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">America</a> could not be conquered.<sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup> Due to his stance, Pitt was very popular amongst the American colonists. This high esteem approached to idolatry according to historian <a href="/wiki/Clinton_Rossiter" title="Clinton Rossiter">Clinton Rossiter</a>:
</p>
<blockquote><p>In the last decade of the colonial period the ideal of the man of public virtue was made real in the person of William Pitt. The cult of this noblest of Whigs, "the Genius of England and the Comet of his Age" was well advanced toward idolatry at least five years before the Stamp Act. The greatest of "the great men of England", the last and noblest of the Romans, was considered the embodiment of virtue, wisdom, patriotism, liberty, and temperance&#160; ... Pitt, "glorious and immortal", the "guardian of America", was the idol of the colonies&#160; ... A <a href="/wiki/Sons_of_Liberty" title="Sons of Liberty">Son of Liberty</a> in Bristol County, Massachusetts, paid him the ultimate tribute of identification with English liberty: "Our toast in general is,—<i>Magna Charta</i>, the <i>British Constitution</i>,—<small>PITT</small> and Liberty forever!"<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg/220px-The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="161" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg/330px-The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg/440px-The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham_by_John_Singleton_Copley.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2400" data-file-height="1760" /></a><figcaption><i><a href="/wiki/The_Death_of_the_Earl_of_Chatham" title="The Death of the Earl of Chatham">The Death of the Earl of Chatham</a></i> in the House of Lords, 7 April 1778.  Painting by <a href="/wiki/John_Singleton_Copley" title="John Singleton Copley">John Singleton Copley</a>, 1779–80. (In fact he died 34 days after the seizure depicted.)</figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Monument_to_William_Pitt,_Earl_of_Chatham,_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Monument_to_William_Pitt%2C_Earl_of_Chatham%2C_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg/220px-Monument_to_William_Pitt%2C_Earl_of_Chatham%2C_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="330" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Monument_to_William_Pitt%2C_Earl_of_Chatham%2C_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg/330px-Monument_to_William_Pitt%2C_Earl_of_Chatham%2C_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/36/Monument_to_William_Pitt%2C_Earl_of_Chatham%2C_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg/440px-Monument_to_William_Pitt%2C_Earl_of_Chatham%2C_Westminster_Abbey_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3456" data-file-height="5184" /></a><figcaption>Monument to Pitt, Westminster Abbey</figcaption></figure>
<p>Pitt had now almost no personal following, mainly owing to the grave mistake he had made in not forming an alliance with the Rockingham party, but his eloquence was as powerful as ever, and all its power was directed against the government policy in the contest with America, which had become the question of all-absorbing interest. His last appearance in the House of Lords was on 7 April 1778, on the occasion of the <a href="/wiki/Charles_Lennox,_3rd_Duke_of_Richmond" title="Charles Lennox, 3rd Duke of Richmond">Duke of Richmond</a>'s motion for an address praying the king to conclude peace with America on any terms.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In view of the hostile demonstrations of France the various parties had come generally to see the necessity of such a measure but Chatham could not brook the thought of a step which implied submission to the "natural enemy" whom it had been the main object of his life to humble, and he declaimed for a considerable time, though with diminished vigour, against the motion. After the Duke of Richmond had replied, he rose again excitedly as if to speak, pressed his hand upon his breast, and fell down in a fit.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> His last words before he collapsed were: "My Lords, any state is better than despair; if we must fall, let us fall like men." <a href="/wiki/James_Harris,_1st_Earl_of_Malmesbury" title="James Harris, 1st Earl of Malmesbury">James Harris</a> MP, however, recorded that <a href="/wiki/Robert_Nugent,_1st_Earl_Nugent" title="Robert Nugent, 1st Earl Nugent">Lord Nugent</a> had told him that Chatham's last words in the Lords were: "If the Americans defend independence, they shall find me in their way" and that his very last words (spoken to <a href="/wiki/John_Pitt,_2nd_Earl_of_Chatham" title="John Pitt, 2nd Earl of Chatham">his soldier son John</a>) were: "Leave your dying father, and go to the defence of your country".<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>He was moved to his home at Hayes, where his middle son <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William</a> read to him <a href="/wiki/Homer" title="Homer">Homer</a>'s passage about the death of <a href="/wiki/Hector" title="Hector">Hector</a>. Chatham died on 11 May 1778, aged 69. Although he was initially buried at Hayes, all parties combined to show their sense of the national loss and the Commons presented an address to the king praying that the deceased statesman might be buried with the honours of a public funeral. A sum was voted for a public monument which was erected over a new grave in <a href="/wiki/Westminster_Abbey" title="Westminster Abbey">Westminster Abbey</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup> The monument, by the sculptor <a href="/wiki/John_Bacon_(sculptor,_born_1740)" title="John Bacon (sculptor, born 1740)">John Bacon</a>, has a figure of Pitt above statues of <a href="/wiki/Britannia" title="Britannia">Britannia</a> and <a href="/wiki/Neptune_(mythology)" title="Neptune (mythology)">Neptune</a> with figures representing Prudence, Fortitude, the Earth and also a sea creature.<sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Guildhall,_London" title="Guildhall, London">Guildhall</a> <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Burke" title="Edmund Burke">Edmund Burke</a>'s inscription summed up what he had meant to the city: he was "the minister by whom commerce was united with and made to flourish by war". Soon after the funeral a bill was passed bestowing a pension of £4,000 a year on his successors in the <a href="/wiki/Earl_of_Chatham" title="Earl of Chatham">earldom</a>. He had a family of three sons and two daughters, of whom the second son, <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William</a>, became Prime Minister in 1783.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Pitt_the_Younger.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Pitt_the_Younger.jpg/170px-Pitt_the_Younger.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="291" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Pitt_the_Younger.jpg/255px-Pitt_the_Younger.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Pitt_the_Younger.jpg/340px-Pitt_the_Younger.jpg 2x" data-file-width="421" data-file-height="721" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a> was to become Prime Minister at a young age and lead Britain for more than twenty years.</figcaption></figure>
<p><a href="/wiki/Horace_Walpole" title="Horace Walpole">Horace Walpole</a>, not an uncritical admirer, wrote of Pitt:
</p>
<blockquote><p>It were ingratitude to him to say that he did not give such a reverberation to our stagnating councils, as exceedingly altered the appearance of our fortune. He warded off the evil hour that seemed approaching, he infused vigour into our arms, he taught the nation to speak again as England used to speak to foreign powers&#160; ... Pitt, on entering upon administration, had found the nation at the lowest ebb in point of power and reputation&#160; ... France, who meant to be feared, was feared heartily&#160; ... They were willing to trust that France would be so good as to ruin us by inches. Pitt had roused us from this ignoble lethargy&#160; ... The admirers of Mr Pitt extol the reverberation he gave to our councils, the despondence he banished, the spirit he infused, the conquests he made, the security he affixed to our trade and plantations, the humiliation of France, the glory of Britain carried under his administration to a pitch at which it never had arrived—and all this is exactly true.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p><a href="/wiki/Samuel_Johnson" title="Samuel Johnson">Samuel Johnson</a> is reported to have said that "Walpole was a minister given by the king to the people, but Pitt was a minister given by the people to the king", and the remark correctly indicated Chatham's distinctive place among English statesmen. He was the first minister whose main strength lay in the support of the nation at large as distinct from its representatives in the Commons, where his personal following was always small. He was the first to discern that public opinion, though generally slow to form and slow to act, is in the end the paramount power in the state; and he was the first to use it not in an emergency merely, but throughout a whole political career.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Chatham marked the commencement of that vast change in the movement of <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_England" title="Politics of England">English politics</a> by which it came about that the sentiment of the great mass of the people then told effectively on the action of the government from day to day—almost from hour to hour. He was well fitted to secure the sympathy and admiration of his countrymen, for his virtues and his failings were alike English. He was often inconsistent, he was generally intractable and overbearing, and he was always pompous and affected to a degree which, as <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Babington_Macaulay,_1st_Baron_Macaulay" class="mw-redirect" title="Thomas Babington Macaulay, 1st Baron Macaulay">Macaulay</a> remarked, seemed scarcely compatible with true greatness.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Of the last quality evidence was furnished in the stilted style of his letters, and in the fact recorded by <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Seward" title="Thomas Seward">Thomas Seward</a> that he never permitted his under-secretaries to sit in his presence. Burke spoke of "some significant, pompous, creeping, explanatory, ambiguous matter, in the true Chathamic style". But these defects were known only to the inner circle of his associates.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>To the outside public he was endeared as a statesman who could do or suffer "nothing base", and who had the rare power of transfusing his own indomitable energy and courage into all who served under him. "A spirited foreign policy" has always been popular in England, and Pitt was the most popular of English ministers, because he was the most successful exponent of such a policy. In domestic affairs his influence was small and almost entirely indirect. He himself confessed his unfitness for dealing with questions of finance. The commercial prosperity that was produced by his war policy was in a great part delusive, as prosperity so produced must always be, though it had permanent effects of the highest moment in the rise of such centres of industry as Glasgow. This, however, was a remote result which he could have neither intended nor foreseen.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115_73-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChisholm19115-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It has been suggested that Pitt was in fact a far more orthodox Whig than has been historically portrayed demonstrated by his sitting for rotten borough seats controlled by aristocratic magnates, and his lifelong concern for protecting the <a href="/wiki/Balance_of_power_in_international_relations" class="mw-redirect" title="Balance of power in international relations">balance of power</a> on the European continent, which marked him out from many other Patriots.<sup id="cite_ref-84" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-84">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Historians<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Words_to_watch#Unsupported_attributions" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Words to watch"><span title="The material near this tag possibly uses too-vague attribution or weasel words. (April 2018)">who?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> have described Pitt as "the greatest British statesman of the eighteenth century".<sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup> He is immortalised in <a href="/wiki/St_Stephen%27s_Hall" class="mw-redirect" title="St Stephen&#39;s Hall">St Stephen's Hall</a>, where he and other notable Parliamentarians look on at visitors to Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-ssstat_86-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ssstat-86">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The American city of <a href="/wiki/Pittsburgh" title="Pittsburgh">Pittsburgh</a>, originally <a href="/wiki/Fort_Duquesne" title="Fort Duquesne">Fort Duquesne</a>, was renamed for Pitt after it was captured from the French during the Seven Years' War.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family_and_personal_life">Family and personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Family and personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Pitt married <a href="/wiki/Hester_Pitt,_Countess_of_Chatham" title="Hester Pitt, Countess of Chatham">Lady Hester Grenville</a> (1720–1803), daughter of the <a href="/wiki/Hester_Temple,_1st_Countess_Temple" class="mw-redirect" title="Hester Temple, 1st Countess Temple">1st Countess Temple</a>, on 16 November 1754 by <a href="/wiki/Francis_Ayscough" title="Francis Ayscough">Francis Ayscough</a> under special licence at her home in <a href="/wiki/Argyle_Street,_London" class="mw-redirect" title="Argyle Street, London">Argyle Street, London</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECokayne143_87-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECokayne143-87">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>They had five children, born at Hayes:<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Hester_Stanhope,_Viscountess_Mahon" title="Hester Stanhope, Viscountess Mahon">Lady Hester Pitt</a> (19 October 1755&#160;&#8211;&#32;20 July 1780), who married <a href="/wiki/Charles_Stanhope,_3rd_Earl_Stanhope" title="Charles Stanhope, 3rd Earl Stanhope">Viscount Mahon</a>, later the 3rd <a href="/wiki/Earl_Stanhope" title="Earl Stanhope">Earl Stanhope</a>, on 19 December 1774; three children, including the traveler and Arabist <a href="/wiki/Lady_Hester_Stanhope" title="Lady Hester Stanhope">Lady Hester Stanhope</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/John_Pitt,_2nd_Earl_of_Chatham" title="John Pitt, 2nd Earl of Chatham">John Pitt, 2nd Earl of Chatham</a> (1756–1835), who married <a href="/wiki/Mary_Pitt,_Countess_of_Chatham" title="Mary Pitt, Countess of Chatham">The Hon. Mary Townshend</a>; no issue</li>
<li>Lady Harriet Pitt (18 April 1758 – 1786),<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup> who married <a href="/wiki/Edward_James_Eliot" title="Edward James Eliot">The Hon. Edward James Eliot</a>, oldest son of the <a href="/wiki/Edward_Craggs-Eliot,_1st_Baron_Eliot" title="Edward Craggs-Eliot, 1st Baron Eliot">1st Baron Eliot</a>, in 1785; one child</li>
<li>Hon. <a href="/wiki/William_Pitt_the_Younger" title="William Pitt the Younger">William Pitt the Younger</a> (28 May 1759 – 23 January 1806), who also served as Prime Minister; never married</li>
<li>Hon. James Charles Pitt (24 April 1761&#160;&#8211;&#32;13 November 1780),<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> officer who died in <a href="/wiki/Antigua" title="Antigua">Antigua</a>; never married</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cultural_references">Cultural references</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Cultural references">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>There have been at least two <a href="/wiki/Royal_Navy" title="Royal Navy">Royal Navy</a> ships that bore the name <a href="/wiki/HMS_Pitt" title="HMS Pitt">HMS <i>Pitt</i></a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Places_named_after_William_Pitt">Places named after William Pitt</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Places named after William Pitt">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1088965794">@media(min-width:720px){.mw-parser-output .columns-start .column{float:left;min-width:20em}.mw-parser-output .columns-2 .column{width:50%}.mw-parser-output .columns-3 .column{width:33.3%}.mw-parser-output .columns-4 .column{width:25%}.mw-parser-output .columns-5 .column{width:20%}}</style><div class="columns-start columns-2"><div class="column">
<p><b>United States</b>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_Strait" title="Chatham Strait">Chatham Strait</a>, Alaska</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham,_Virginia" title="Chatham, Virginia">Chatham, Virginia</a><sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham,_New_Hampshire" title="Chatham, New Hampshire">Chatham, New Hampshire</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham,_New_Jersey" title="Chatham, New Jersey">Chatham, New Jersey</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_County,_Georgia" title="Chatham County, Georgia">Chatham County, Georgia</a><sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_Square_(Savannah,_Georgia)" title="Chatham Square (Savannah, Georgia)">Chatham Square</a>, Savannah, Georgia</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pitt_County,_North_Carolina" title="Pitt County, North Carolina">Pitt County, North Carolina</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_County,_North_Carolina" title="Chatham County, North Carolina">Chatham County, North Carolina</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsburg,_New_Hampshire" title="Pittsburg, New Hampshire">Pittsburg, New Hampshire</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsburgh" title="Pittsburgh">Pittsburgh</a>, Pennsylvania (renamed, previously called <a href="/wiki/Fort_Duquesne" title="Fort Duquesne">Fort Duquesne</a>). After British General <a href="/wiki/John_Forbes_(British_Army_officer)" title="John Forbes (British Army officer)">John Forbes</a> occupied <a href="/wiki/Fort_Duquesne" title="Fort Duquesne">Fort Duquesne</a> during the <a href="/wiki/French_and_Indian_War" title="French and Indian War">French and Indian War</a>, he ordered the site's reconstruction and named it after then-Secretary of State Pitt. He also named the settlement between the rivers "Pittsborough", which would eventually become known as Pittsburgh.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_University" title="Chatham University">Chatham University</a>, in Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham,_Massachusetts" title="Chatham, Massachusetts">Chatham, Massachusetts</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsfield,_Massachusetts" title="Pittsfield, Massachusetts">Pittsfield, Massachusetts</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsfield,_New_Hampshire" title="Pittsfield, New Hampshire">Pittsfield, New Hampshire</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsfield_Charter_Township,_Michigan" title="Pittsfield Charter Township, Michigan">Pittsfield Charter Township, Michigan</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsgrove_Township,_New_Jersey" title="Pittsgrove Township, New Jersey">Pittsgrove Township, New Jersey</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittstown,_New_Jersey" title="Pittstown, New Jersey">Pittstown, New Jersey</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittston,_Pennsylvania" title="Pittston, Pennsylvania">Pittston, Pennsylvania</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittston_Township,_Luzerne_County,_Pennsylvania" class="mw-redirect" title="Pittston Township, Luzerne County, Pennsylvania">Pittston Township, Pennsylvania</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsylvania_County,_Virginia" title="Pittsylvania County, Virginia">Pittsylvania County, Virginia</a>, and its county seat, <a href="/wiki/Chatham,_Virginia" title="Chatham, Virginia">Chatham</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Pittsboro,_North_Carolina" title="Pittsboro, North Carolina">Pittsboro, North Carolina</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_Square" title="Chatham Square">Chatham Square</a>, <a href="/wiki/New_York_City" title="New York City">New York City</a></li>
<li>The short-lived, sparsely-colonised, extralegal colony of <a href="/wiki/Vandalia_(colony)" title="Vandalia (colony)">Vandalia</a> (located in modern West Virginia and named after <a href="/wiki/Queen_Charlotte_of_Mecklenburg-Strelitz" class="mw-redirect" title="Queen Charlotte of Mecklenburg-Strelitz">Queen Charlotte of Mecklenburg-Strelitz</a>) was originally named Pittsylvania after Pitt.</li></ul>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1088965794"></div><div class="column">
<p><b>Canada</b>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Brownsburg-Chatham,_Quebec" class="mw-redirect" title="Brownsburg-Chatham, Quebec">Chatham Township</a>, Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-CTQ-Cm_94-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CTQ-Cm-94">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Chatham,_Ontario" class="mw-redirect" title="Chatham, Ontario">Chatham, Ontario</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/CFB_Chatham" title="CFB Chatham">CFB Chatham</a>, near <a href="/wiki/Chatham,_New_Brunswick" title="Chatham, New Brunswick">Chatham, New Brunswick</a></li></ul>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1088965794"></div><div class="column">
<p><b>Australia</b>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Pitt_Town,_New_South_Wales" title="Pitt Town, New South Wales">Pitt Town, New South Wales</a>, named after Pitt by <a href="/wiki/Governor_Macquarie" class="mw-redirect" title="Governor Macquarie">Governor Macquarie</a> in 1810</li>
<li><a href="/w/index.php?title=Chatham,_New_South_Wales&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Chatham, New South Wales (page does not exist)">Chatham, New South Wales</a></li></ul>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1088965794"></div><div class="column">
<p><b>Ecuador</b>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/San_Crist%C3%B3bal_Island" title="San Cristóbal Island">Chatham Island</a>, Galápagos</li></ul>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1088965794"></div><div class="column">
<p><b>New Zealand</b>
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Chatham_Islands" title="Chatham Islands">Chatham Islands</a> an archipelago including <a href="/wiki/Pitt_Island" title="Pitt Island">Pitt Island</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chatham_Island" title="Chatham Island">Chatham Island</a></li></ul>
</div><div style="clear: both"></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="References_in_popular_culture">References in popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=William_Pitt,_1st_Earl_of_Chatham&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: References in popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>Pitt is referred to in the <i><a href="/wiki/The_Simpsons" title="The Simpsons">Simpsons</a></i> episode "<a href="/wiki/Homer_at_the_Bat" title="Homer at the Bat">Homer at the Bat</a>", where <a href="/wiki/Barney_Gumble" title="Barney Gumble">Barney</a> and guest star <a href="/wiki/Wade_Boggs" title="Wade Boggs">Wade Boggs</a> get into a bar fight after a heated debate over the subject of who was England's greatest Prime Minister. Boggs claims Pitt the Elder was the greatest Prime Minister to Barney's <a href="/wiki/Lord_Palmerston" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Palmerston">Lord Palmerston</a>, causing Barney to punch Boggs in the face, knocking him unconscious.</li>
<li>Pitt is briefly derided (but does not appear) in the <i>Blackadder The Third</i> episode "<a href="/wiki/Dish_and_Dishonesty" class="mw-redirect" title="Dish and Dishonesty">Dish and Dishonesty</a>". Blackadder states that he is "about as effective as a catflap in an elephant house".</li>
<li>In <i><a href="/wiki/The_Two_Georges" title="The Two Georges">The Two Georges</a></i>, an <a href="/wiki/Alternate_history" title="Alternate history">alternate history</a> by <a href="/wiki/Harry_Turtledove" title="Harry Turtledove">Harry Turtledove</a>, William Pitt was the Prime Minister during a period of political tension between <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Great_Britain" title="Kingdom of Great Britain">Great Britain</a> and its North American colonies. He played a significant role in easing those tensions and ensuring the colonists remained content British subjects. He was also one of the historical figures in <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Gainsborough" title="Thomas Gainsborough">Thomas Gainsborough</a>'s fictional painting <i>The Two Georges</i>, depicting <a href="/wiki/George_Washington" title="George Washington">George Washington</a> being presented to King <a href="/wiki/George_III" title="George III">George III</a>.</li>
<li>In 1770, <a href="/wiki/Laurence_Sterne" title="Laurence Sterne">Laurence Sterne</a> dedicated his masterpiece, <i><a href="/wiki/The_Life_and_Opinions_of_Tristram_Shandy,_Gentleman" title="The Life and Opinions of Tristram Shandy, Gentleman">The Life and Opinions of Tristram Shandy, Gentleman</a></i> to the rt. hon. Mr Pitt.<sup id="cite_ref-95" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-95">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 1790, Scotland's Bard, the poet <a href="/wiki/Robert_Burns" title="Robert Burns">Robert Burns</a>, referred to Pitt in his <a href="/wiki/Scots-language_literature" title="Scots-language literature">Scots language</a> poem, <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.robertburns.org/works/299.shtml">"Lines To A Gentleman"</a>, which Burns composed in response to being sent a newspaper, which the gentlemen sender offered to continue providing free of charge. The poet's satirical summary of events makes clear that he has no interest in the reports contained in the newspaper. The poem's section on events in Parliament, which refers to Pitt as 'Chatham Will', is as follows:</li></ul>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>How royal George, the Lord leuk o'er him!<br />
Was managing St. Stephen's quorum; <br />
If sleekit Chatham Will was livin, <br />
Or glaikit Charlie got his nieve in; <br />
How daddie Burke the plea was cookin, <br />
If Warren Hasting's neck was yeukin; <br />
How cesses, stents, and fees were rax'd. <br />
Or if bare arses yet were tax'd...
</p>
</div></blockquote>

<li id="cite_note-65"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-65">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">"Two [...] of his diversions, against the French settlements in West Africa, were materially, as well as strategically, most successful. Both beautifully planned by Pitt and admirably carried out, the first under Captains Marsh and Mason, the second under Commodore Keppel, another of Pitt's boys in the thirties, resulted in the capture of all the French factories on that coast, including Goree and Senegal."
